Covered with the waits run inline (a removed seat's waits stop at the reload while a remaining seat's go on); a thread still blocked in `agent wait` across a reload was not tested on real threads.
- **`--log` mode** never re-reads the file, as before; README says to restart that watcher after an edit.
- **Tests.** `tests/test_section26_6.py` (8 tests); four expectations of 0.13.0's tests changed for the marker and the contact page header (`test_section24.py`: the 162x8 contact row, the packed contact, the built contact row; `test_section25.py`: the contact page title twice), and the fleet page test reads the new `layout` line. The suite has 851.

## 27. Setup instructions and external contact capacity (24 Oct 2026)

This section corrects public setup instructions and explicit contact visibility. A board must support a combined seat/contact roster without silently hiding contacts; the capacity fixtures use synthetic agents. No automatic discovery, external-fleet editing or agent control is added.

### 27.1 Setup and documentation

- README installation must work from a GitHub install without assuming a source checkout. Resolve the enabled `yarrasys.fleet` plugin's `manifest_path` from `herdr plugin list --json`, derive its root, run its `bin/herdr-fleet install-cli`, and explain `~/.local/bin` on PATH. Keep a separate local-development path. Check syntax against the installed binary and the official plugin documentation.
- The quick start explicitly enters the target repository root before `init` and editing `.herdr/fleet.toml`. Explain installed agent executables, authentication, integrations, inside-Herdr requirements, dry runs, startup dialogs and adopting an existing orchestrator. Do not require optional Claude hooks for a working fleet.
- Audit the entire README against the CLI, implementation and latest design amendments, including commands, options, examples, contacts, board keys, reloads, delivery modes, costs, safety and limitations. Correct stale claims such as three example seats, no curses UI, and `pane zoom --current`; the board names its own pane explicitly (25.7). Distinguish local polling from agent turns, explicit delivery overrides from normal FYI delivery, and successful kickoffs from unconfirmed prompts.
- The generated starter places the top-level `watch = false` example before any table so enabling the layout example cannot silently turn it into `layout.watch`. Comments explain mutually exclusive alternatives. Correct stale setup comments in the development fleet, agent guidance and shipped examples without changing the first three AGENTS.md rules.
- `docs/testing.md` must distinguish current instructions from historical release coverage. Provide portable unit-test instructions alongside checks for both interpreter paths. Replace stale or unsafe copyable live procedures with a current scratch-fleet procedure, explicit targets, state checks and manual owner interaction for typing and dialogs. Retain historical evidence in this design document; do not claim an unrun live check passed.

### 27.2 Contact visibility

- Contacts are explicit `[[contacts]]` declarations with exact-name and optional cwd/kind matching. Local development configuration is outside the public tree. Editing that roster never grants control over external agents.
- Board capacity and packing count seats and contacts together. When all rows fit, show seats followed by contacts in their existing order. When they fit packed, pack the combined ordered list; every contact retains its marker, style, click target and page. When neither fits, prefer seats using the existing seat urgency order, use remaining capacity for contacts in file order, and count every hidden row in `+N more`. A contact remains reachable through Left/Right from a seat page even when its row is hidden. Very short boards keep their existing most-urgent-seat behavior.
- Board-mode fleet-file reload applies contact edits within a refresh. Verify matching with read-only data; adding a contact requires no focus, zoom, keys or prompt. Running Python code changes still require a new watcher process.

### 27.3 Verification

Add a regression test that enables the generated layout example together with top-level `watch = false` and loads the resulting fleet without warnings, with the watcher disabled. Add meaningful board tests for three seats plus five contacts at normal, packed and narrow sizes, including overflow counts and opening a packed contact page. Run both interpreter suites and compileall. Review the resulting diff against this section. Smoke-test generated setup in a temporary directory and the board on a cheap Claude scratch seat before committing; record the actual results in an amendment. No push is authorized.

### 27.4 Amendments and verification

- **Implementation.** The shared `board.plan` now counts seats and contacts together; rendering, selection and hit testing continue to use that one plan. Combined file order is preserved when every row fits; overflow keeps the existing seat urgency order before contacts. Below three rows the most-urgent-seat view is unchanged. `scaffold.STARTER_FLEET` places the top-level watcher boolean before all tables. The only other runtime-source edit is documentation: `send --when-ready` help now says an unready target stays queued, with `--now` refusing, and the send description and `--fyi` help explain explicit delivery overrides instead of promising FYIs never wake a seat. Delivery behavior, dependencies, key paths and version strings are unchanged.
- **Documentation.** README installation resolves the exact enabled plugin id and guards launcher installation when lookup fails. It separates agent installation/login, Herdr integrations and optional inbox hooks; explains PATH, repository root, adoption, dry runs, watcher updates, contacts and troubleshooting; and corrects stale safety, board, cost and delivery claims. The testing guide replaces historical copyable procedures with current fake-test and disposable-fleet instructions, and points here for historical evidence. AGENTS.md's first three rules are byte-for-byte unchanged; its existing Ctrl-U and own-pane zoom descriptions now match 23.11 and 25.7.
- **Regression evidence (builder).** Ten new tests in `tests/test_section27.py`; eight failed against the old implementation and two controls passed. Failures covered the starter's ignored `layout.watch`, contacts not triggering packing, missing hidden-row counts, packed click mapping and contact-page navigation. All ten pass with the change; two section-24 expectations now describe packing instead of silently hidden contacts. The full suite grows from 851 to 861 tests.
- **Historical automated checks.** Both Python 3.9 and Python 3.12 full suites passed 861 tests, and compilation passed. An initial concurrent run failed the existing `RealThreads.test_waiters_in_threads_deliver_and_stop_cleanly` on a missing second-delivery output line. All 23 watcher tests then passed in isolation and a serial full rerun passed; the watcher implementation and thread test were not changed. This intermittent result remains a verification limit.
- **Historical setup verification.** The enabled-plugin root lookup and temporary launcher were exercised under bash and zsh against an existing installation. Create/keep initializer behavior and the layout/disabled-watcher example were checked under both test interpreters. Shell, TOML/JSON and local-link checks passed. Plugin installation/update/link and integration installation were not repeated.
- **Historical live verification.** A disposable Claude seat was started, briefed and cleaned up. A read-only board model resolved configured external contacts; rendering covered full, packed and narrow layouts with hidden-row counts. The checks made no external-agent prompt, key, focus or zoom request. Deployment names, paths, pane ids, model choice and roster are omitted; the names in examples are synthetic.
- **Historical running-watcher check.** Contact-file edits were observed through an existing watcher reload. That did not install the new packing code into the running process. Live mouse clicks, hooks, input holds, dialogs and watcher process replacement were not retested in this change.
- **Historical independent review.** Review found no actionable issue, and independently passed both 861-test suites, compilation, Python 3.8 grammar parsing and documentation checks. Substituting the previous plan/starter reproduced eight failures and two passing controls. The earlier threaded-test output race did not reproduce in the review suites. This records completed checks, not authorization to release, install or push.

## 28. Public documentation and repository privacy

The owner requested validation that the documentation is suitable for a public audience and exposes no internal organization information. The publication boundary is the entire tracked source tree, including historical design notes, examples, fixtures and development configuration. A clean current tree does not remove information from older Git commits.

### 28.1 Publication boundary

- Keep public package identity: the repository owner/source, `yarrasys.fleet`, manifest action ids, licence copyright and third-party attribution. These identify the published software; they are not deployment details.
- Replace actual product/fleet names, developer usernames and absolute home/work paths, branch/deployment references, copied message bodies, internal commit/message ids and operational rollout notes with explicitly synthetic examples or product-level explanations. Preserve documented behavior, safety invariants, numbered sections and technical rationale. Historical records must identify anonymized examples as such rather than claiming synthetic details were observed live.
- Rename the shipped example to `examples/team` and document `init --example team`. It is a generic orchestrator/developer/reviewer/designer team with no organization-specific project assumptions, deployment authority, unavailable role document or hard-coded model requirement. Adjust CLI help and fixtures consistently; no legacy internal-name alias is published.
- Keep the actual development fleet, contact roster, kickoff prompts and state note on disk for the existing local workflow, but remove root `.herdr/` files from Git tracking and ignore `/.herdr/` at the repository root. Shipped example `.herdr/` trees remain tracked. Do not edit, restart or prompt any live fleet for this cleanup.
- Source comments and tests are public too: use synthetic fixture names and paths there. Preserve real public upstream links and public plugin identifiers; do not blindly delete copyright, attribution or third-party credits.

### 28.2 Verification and history

Inventory all tracked files and scan their contents and names for known internal identifiers, user home/work paths, copied operational text and credential patterns. Inspect findings in context and maintain a narrow allowlist for public package identity and licence attribution. Check the generic example initializes and loads, its kickoff references exist, all documentation snippets and local links remain valid, and both required interpreter suites plus compileall pass. An independent reviewer checks the full publication boundary.

Report historical exposure separately using read-only Git inspection. The owner chose removal from the current files with existing history unchanged. Do not rewrite, remove or force-push history, and do not prepare a replacement history. Record actual validation and limitations here before committing. No push is authorized.

### 28.3 Verification and limits

- **Publication boundary.** The candidate contains 94 files. Seven checkout-local fleet/configuration files were removed from Git tracking and protected by the root-only `/.herdr/` ignore rule. Their local contents were verified unchanged; the generic example's `.herdr/` files remain publishable. Runtime contact configuration and running fleets were not changed by this cleanup.
- **Public content.** All candidate filenames and contents were scanned for known deployment identities, personal/organization work paths, copied operational phrases, internal release references and credential signatures. No matches remained after cleanup. Additional literal-authentication and private-network-reference checks also returned no findings. These are bounded scans plus content review, not a guarantee about unknown identifiers or older commits. Private scan patterns and local backup locations are not part of the public repository.
- **Examples and historical notes.** `init --example team` supplies four generic roles with empty native arguments, local kickoff templates and explicit owner authorization for publishing actions. The example initialized seven files and kept all seven on a repeat under both test interpreters; the fleet loaded without warnings. Historical design/requirements/proposal notes now distinguish technical history from synthetic example data and omit deployment anecdotes, rosters and copied operational plans.
- **Documentation checks.** Across the complete publication candidate, 18 shell blocks passed both bash and zsh syntax checks, 11 TOML and six JSON blocks parsed, and all 13 local Markdown link/anchor references resolved. The package/repository identity, copyright and third-party attribution were preserved intentionally.
- **History and live limits.** Existing commits still retain previously recorded material. History was not rewritten, and no publication, release or push was performed. No new live-agent smoke test was needed for this content cleanup; initialization and fake-agent tests cover the renamed example, while existing live fleets remain untouched.

- **Automated regression checks.** Both required interpreter suites pass 862 tests and compilation passes. The added team-example test initializes and renders all four roles, requires no fixed model arguments or external role document, and fails against the previous example directory in an isolated baseline copy. All 40 runtime modules retain the same executable syntax trees after excluding docstrings; only the shipped example selection/data changes. The first three AGENTS.md rules and version files are unchanged.
- **Independent review.** The reviewer returned MERGE with no actionable findings after checking the complete publication boundary and final wording corrections. Independent checks covered the privacy scans, Python 3.8 grammar, all example-role rendering and publishing constraints, documentation syntax/links, the preserved safety rules and runtime logic, and retained local configuration. Full-suite and compilation results above are the builder's evidence; the reviewer performed no live-agent operations and did not repeat those mutating checks. Historical exposure remains a separate, accepted limit.

## 29. Codex context usage and window

The owner reported that context appears for Claude seats but not Codex. Both `context_size.seat_context` and the board cache deliberately accept Claude transcripts only. A synthetic Codex rollout with a valid usage event reproduces the blank reading. This section adds Codex support to the same read-only surfaces; it does not query an account limit or make a model request.

### 29.1 Verified data and meaning

The installed Codex CLI's generated protocol schema exposes last-request usage, cumulative usage and an optional model context window. Official [Codex app-server documentation](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/app-server) describes `thread/tokenUsage/updated` notifications and persisted JSONL thread logs. The on-disk event format below was verified locally by extracting usage numbers and schema keys only; no live prompts, session identities, file paths or deployment readings are published here. It is an observed local format, not a promise that every future Codex version persists the same schema.

```json
{"timestamp":"2026-01-01T00:00:01Z","type":"event_msg","payload":{"type":"token_count","info":{"last_token_usage":{"input_tokens":120000,"cached_input_tokens":100000,"output_tokens":3000,"reasoning_output_tokens":1000,"total_tokens":123000},"total_token_usage":{"total_tokens":9000000},"model_context_window":200000}}}
```

- Context usage is the most recent valid `info.last_token_usage.total_tokens`, 123000 in this synthetic example. Never use cumulative `total_token_usage`; never add cached input or reasoning output again. This is Codex's last reported reading and can change after compaction or a model/configuration change.
- The window is that same event's `info.model_context_window` when it is a positive integer. It is the effective reported context window, not a hard-coded model catalogue value, subscription quota or weekly rate limit. Missing/invalid window means usage-only display, not an estimated limit.
- A missing/null `info` (for example a rate-limit-only event), corrupt/partial JSON, missing usage or a non-integer/negative/boolean usage value is skipped. A valid zero usage value is retained, so a reset is not replaced with an older pre-reset reading. Invalid window values are omitted without discarding valid usage. The latest valid event wins, including a lower post-compaction usage/window.

### 29.2 Discovery, privacy and caching

- Match a live agent's `agent_session.source == "herdr:codex"` and a validated plain session id. Locate only that session's `rollout-*-<session id>.jsonl` under the active sessions root: `$HERDR_FLEET_CODEX_SESSIONS` when set, else `$CODEX_HOME/sessions`, else `$HOME/.codex/sessions`. Support the observed year/month/day layout and a root-level rollout. Escape the root, reject unsafe ids and select the newest match when duplicates exist; do not inspect unrelated message bodies or read an index/database to infer a session.
- Read backward in 256 KiB chunks, at most 4 MiB, as for Claude. Only a correctly typed token-count event contributes data. Keep only usage/window numbers, timestamp and a source tag; no prompt, tool content, rate-limit details, transcript path or session id is returned, printed or logged. Nothing in either agent's source directories is written. This extends invariant 8's transcript-source boundary to Codex and preserves invariant 9.
- Share provider resolution and reading between `status --context` and the board cache. Cache keys include provider, root and session id to prevent collisions; cache successful readings by path, modification time and size, rereading only when changed. A missing session/file is retried on a later refresh, and changing the session, root or provider invalidates the relevant cache. Missing/unreadable/unsupported data remains unavailable; it is not zero.
- Existing Claude lookup, token calculation, JSON shape, labels and cache behavior remain valid. Without `status --context`, status reads no agent transcript. No extra Herdr call is introduced by context reading. Other agent kinds remain unsupported unless their verified format is added in another design section.

### 29.3 Status and board

- Codex `status --json --context` returns `{"tokens":123000,"at":"2026-01-01T00:00:01Z","source":"codex-transcript","window_tokens":200000}`. Omit `window_tokens` when unavailable. Claude JSON remains unchanged. `restart_hint_tokens` compares the reported used tokens as before; no new automatic threshold, restart or warning policy is introduced.
- Status text uses `123k/200k (2 min)` when both are known, usage with age when only usage is known, and `-` when unavailable. Labels use the existing token abbreviation rules.
- Board rows and seat-page headers show `ctx 123k/200k` when both are known, `ctx 183k` without a window, and blank without a reading. The board keeps the optional window in its row model. Normal rows reserve 16 columns for context when any visible row has a known window, consistently across those rows; otherwise keep the existing 10-column layout. Existing width breakpoints, compact packed rows and hidden-row behavior stay as specified in sections 24–27. The seat page provides the complete context reading even where compact rows omit it. Keep existing threshold markers without truncating the synthetic used/window example.
- README, test guide and agent architecture guidance describe both providers and these limits using synthetic examples. Retain section 28's public-content boundary. Do not add live session data or internal configuration to public files.

### 29.4 Verification

Add a failing Codex fixture test before implementation. Cover metadata/id validation, default/override roots, root/day layouts and duplicates, bounded backward reads, partial/corrupt/non-usage events, null info, integer/window validation and zero, cumulative-versus-last usage, cache/reasoning counts not double-counted, compaction/model-window changes, missing source data and privacy whitelisting. Integrate the same fixture through status JSON/text and the real board cache/model/rendering and seat page; verify thresholds and missing data, no transcript read without the status flag, provider/session/root cache isolation, unchanged-file cache hits and refreshed readings after edits, and unchanged Claude behavior.

Run both required interpreter suites, compileall and the public-content checks. Obtain independent review. The designer verifies usage-only reads against a live Codex session and compares the returned readings with the reported numeric fields without prompting or changing that agent; record generic outcomes, not live identities or transcript content. This is not a release or an installation, and no push is authorized.

### 29.5 Verification and limits

- **Regression evidence.** Before implementation, three synthetic integration tests failed for the reader, status and real board cache/model/rendering. The final section adds 31 tests covering the reader, cache, status, source privacy and layout. The transient-read recovery regression also failed before its fix for both providers, including a failure after an earlier successful reading. The cache now retains successful readings only and retries failures even when file metadata is unchanged.
- **Live source verification.** The designer compared the working-tree reader and real board cache with independently extracted latest usage/window fields for three live Codex sessions. Both matched, including a repeated unchanged-file cache read. The final comparison passed after the cache correction. Only generic pass/fail and the number of sessions were emitted; no live identity, path, transcript content or token reading was published. The check sent no prompt or key and did not change any agent, pane, fleet configuration or source transcript.
- **Compatibility and privacy.** Claude's existing token calculation and status JSON remain unchanged. Invalid timestamps now preserve the usage/window display without an age instead of raising an exception. The 95-file publication candidate passes the private-identifier and credential-pattern audit with zero findings; generic example initialization passes under both test interpreters. Documentation shell/JSON/TOML syntax and local links pass, and the first three AGENTS.md safety rules remain unchanged.
- **Automated checks.** The builder's final `/usr/bin/python3 -m unittest discover -s tests` run passes 893 tests under Python 3.9.6; `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.12 -m unittest discover -s tests` passes the same 893 under Python 3.12.13. Both report zero failures, errors or skips, increasing coverage from 862 to 893 cases. `/usr/bin/python3 -m compileall -q herdr_fleet bin/herdr-fleet` and `git diff --check` pass. All 76 Python files parse with Python 3.8 grammar; an actual Python 3.8 interpreter was not run. The builder's complete documentation inventory validates 18 shell blocks under bash and zsh, 11 TOML blocks, eight JSON blocks and local Markdown links/anchors.
- **Independent review.** The reviewer returned MERGE on the final implementation, regression tests and amendment with no actionable findings remaining. Independent checks reproduced recovery from a real reader failure for both providers, verified successful cache hits, exercised layout and backward reads, checked the safety-rule and navigation boundaries, and repeated the public-content, grammar and documentation checks. The reviewer relied on the builder's full-suite/compilation results and the designer's live numeric comparison rather than repeating those checks or accessing live transcripts.
- **Operational limits.** Codex requires matching Herdr session metadata and a readable active rollout using the observed event format. Missing data remains unavailable and an absent window is never inferred. This live check verifies source compatibility and the board cache, not a replacement running strip or every Codex version. Installed code, running watchers, release/version files and Git history were not changed; no installation or push was performed.

## 30. Daily timers enqueue ordinary messages (0.15.0)

The owner requested migration of session-bound recurring prompts into the fleet watcher. A configured timer generates an ordinary queued request for a local seat; scheduling invokes no model and never manages an agent. This section authorizes the implementation, tests and a synthetic scratch-fleet smoke test. Deployment configuration belongs to the requesting coordinator. Do not exercise a production job, change an owner's fleet file, interrupt its coordinator, install globally or push without the separate instruction for that action.

### 30.1 Configuration and scope

```toml
[[timers]]
name = "daily-check"
to = "sample-orchestrator"
file = ".herdr/timers/daily-check.md"
at = "23:07"
tz = "Australia/Sydney"
starts_at = "2026-10-26T00:00:00+11:00"
until = "2026-11-04T09:00:00+11:00"
grace = "6h"
enabled = true
```

- Daily wall-clock schedules only, not general cron, shell execution or external contacts. `name` is a unique plain identifier using the seat-name grammar. `to` must name a declared seat in this fleet, including an adopted seat. Agent kind is irrelevant. `file` is a nonempty UTF-8 prompt file relative to the fleet repository or an absolute path, resolved as other fleet paths are. Empty bodies are errors, never empty prompts. Snapshot the body only when its occurrence is actually written to the mailbox; later edits affect future occurrences.
- `at` is strict 24-hour `HH:MM`. `tz` is a valid available IANA timezone (including `UTC`). `starts_at` and `until` are required offset-aware ISO timestamps, with `until > starts_at`; schedule instants satisfy `starts_at <= scheduled < until`. `grace` defaults to `6h`, accepts the existing positive integer m/h/d duration grammar, and is at most `24h`. `enabled` defaults true and is strictly boolean. Unknown keys warn consistently with the fleet parser. Invalid timer configuration produces the ordinary accumulated fleet-file errors; a prompt file that disappears after validation yields an observable timer error, not a blank message.
- A watcher with configured timers or existing timer history must detect and reconcile valid fleet-file edits in both board and log/non-terminal modes, including removal of the final timer. Reuse a common mtime/reload path rather than competing loaders. An invalid edit retains the last valid fleet and emits a clear error, as the existing board does; it does not count as a cancellation. A never-configured, history-free log watcher retains its existing no-reload contract: restart it when adding its first timer. Timer name is a persistent identity: normal edits, disable/re-enable and removal/re-addition do not erase its occurrence history. A schedule, timezone, target or file edit affects future decisions; do not replay already decided scheduled instants. On removal, disable or a target change, expire that timer's still-pending message under its mailbox lock before allowing a replacement; do not discard unrelated mail. A new name is a deliberately new timer.
- Public examples and fixtures use synthetic roles and prompts. No deployment names, private schedule/job text or personal paths enter tracked files. Increment both version declarations to 0.15.0 for the reviewed candidate; do not tag, publish, install or push it as part of implementation.

### 30.2 Calendar and clock

- Compare aware UTC wall-clock instants for due times and expiry. Keep monotonic time for waiter/debounce/retry intervals. Check timers on watcher start and every normal cycle, including the cycle after sleep or a clock jump. Fire at minute precision with normal watcher-cycle/debounce latency; this is not a real-time scheduler and cannot wake a sleeping computer.
- Resolve daily local dates independently of the machine's local timezone. At an ambiguous daylight-saving wall time, choose its earliest UTC instant and fire once for that local date. At a nonexistent local time, skip and report the gap. A clock moving backward never replays a decided occurrence; moving forward follows catch-up rules. Test Sydney's seasonal transitions and a non-hour-offset zone.
- Preserve Python 3.8+ and the dependency-free macOS/Linux contract. Prefer standard-library `zoneinfo` where present. On Python 3.8, use an isolated short-lived Python subprocess with a validated system-zoneinfo name and its own `TZ`/`time.tzset`, never mutate the multithreaded watcher's global timezone. Candidate wall times must round-trip and ambiguous candidates choose the earliest UTC result. Batch/cache a bounded calendar window so the fallback does not spawn a process every two-second cycle. Do not silently treat an unavailable zone as UTC. Both paths cross one calendar interface and receive the same calendar tests; no external package, model request or shell interpolation.

### 30.3 Durable occurrence and catch-up decisions

- Put timer progress in ignored `.herdr/timers.state.json`, independently of `fleet.state.json`, with a version and per-name history. Use an exclusive timer lock and atomic state replacement; preserve incompatible/corrupt state and fail closed with a clear diagnostic instead of resetting it and replaying jobs. All commands, reload paths and watcher instances obey the same lock ordering: timer lock before any mailbox lock, never acquire the timer lock while already holding a mailbox lock. The ordinary watcher singleton remains in place.
- An occurrence has a stable identity `(timer name, scheduled UTC instant)` and one chosen message id retained durably before enqueue. A prepared record contains metadata only, never a prompt body. Recovery checks that id in both `new/` and `cur/`; an existing message is never created again, including after it was delivered, expired, acknowledged, read or dropped. Recover a crash before file creation, after atomic file creation and after logging without producing a second mailbox message or duplicate logical send. Use the recorded association or deterministic id with collision detection; a conflicting existing message fails closed. A missing prepared message may be created only while that occurrence is still eligible. Persist terminal decisions so edits/restarts do not replay them.
- On first start and after downtime, choose at most the latest undecided due occurrence within the inclusive start/exclusive end range. Enqueue it only while `now < min(scheduled + grace, until)`. If no occurrence is eligible, skip and log the missed interval; older missed runs are logged as skipped, possibly as a summarized interval. Never enqueue a stack of missed days. Before `starts_at`, do nothing. First enabling a timer can catch up within its stated start/grace, so migration must choose an explicit start that does not overlap the previous trigger.
- Keep at most one pending mailbox message per timer. Supersede/expire an older pending occurrence before enqueueing a later eligible one, using the same mailbox lock as delivery. Each queued message's ordinary `ttl` is `min(scheduled + grace, until)`. Expire held messages even when their seat is blocked, absent or busy; the existing delivery engine checks expiry before submission. A prompt already submitted before expiry cannot be recalled, and a long-running receiving job may finish after the timer's cutoff.
- At or after `until`, create no new occurrence, expire any undelivered timer message and record the timer's stopped transition once. Missing files and recoverable write errors produce a visible error and can retry the same prepared occurrence within its eligibility; never advance past an unwritten occurrence silently, send a different body as a duplicate message, or retain a partial public state file.
- This guarantees deduplicated timer enqueueing under the tested recovery points, not exactly-once model execution or external side effects. Preserve existing `sent`/`unconfirmed` behavior: no automatic re-prompt of unconfirmed delivery. A receiver should make the job idempotent for its scheduled date. Do not alter generic delivery guarantees or add retries after an ambiguous prompt result in this feature.

### 30.4 Mailbox, audit and watcher integration

- Timer messages use `from: owner`, `fyi: false`, `queued_by: queue`, the ordinary immutable body/file handling and TTL, plus optional `timer` and `scheduled_for` metadata. This is the owner's declared recurring request, not an invented managed timer agent. Ordinary reply debt is owed to `owner`; normal replies/acks settle it. Include timer name and occurrence in audit records and rendered timer-message headers so the receiver can identify the scheduled date. Bodies remain verbatim in inbox files and are never interpreted to schedule or authorize anything.
- Record the ordinary queued `send` and later ordinary `delivered`/`expired`/`dropped` entries, retaining optional timer metadata through those paths. Add timer decision records for skipped/error/stopped/superseded decisions, with occurrence/message id where known and concise reasons, never copied prompt bodies. Deduplicate logical event logging during recovery by stable occurrence/event identity. Keep generic log and debt parsing compatible with unrelated decision records.
- The scheduler itself only writes queue/state/log metadata; it never calls `safe_prompt`, `send --now`, keys, focus, pane commands, agent start or restart. The foreground watcher evaluates scheduling before discovering queued mailboxes. Its existing waiter, debounce, fresh state/presence checks and `messages.prompt_mailbox` alone deliver timer messages. `watch --once` evaluates due timers then performs its normal delivery pass under the same timer lock; two processes cannot enqueue the same occurrence.
- Expose a concise `timer NAME waiting: blocked|working|unknown|absent|typing` outcome using existing observed delivery states/presence outcomes, without prompting or polling a model. Do not read extra agent screen content to diagnose timers. Retain mailbox TTL enforcement for every delivery path, including pull/hooks/kickoff/plain-send flush, so those paths cannot revive an expired timer request.
- A fleet with neither configured timers nor existing timer journal/history has unchanged watcher/delivery behavior and no timer state/lock/calendar subprocess. Existing history permits reconciliation of removed/disabled timers even when no timers remain configured; this cleanup does not need a calendar subprocess. Preserve section 7 invariants; automatic scheduling is an explicitly configured watcher operation and extends invariant 11's origin, not its allowed target states or delivery path. It sends no additional keys and never manages a seat.

### 30.5 Inspection and handover

- Add `herdr-fleet timers [NAME...] [--json] [--dry-run] [--next N] [--fleet PATH]`. Unknown requested names are usage errors. `--next` defaults 3, range 1–20. No timers prints a useful empty result. Listing/dry-run do not create directories, locks, queues, logs or state, take a write lock, rename panes or deliver anything.
- Show name, target, timezone, enabled/stopped/error/waiting state, next scheduled UTC instant and local display, last occurrence/decision time and outcome, pending message id/deadline, and upcoming schedule instants (with no payload body). JSON represents these explicitly; unavailable values are null, not fabricated. Read existing journal/message metadata and log progress to distinguish enqueued from delivered/expired/unconfirmed. Normal listing may make one read-only `agent list` to explain pending states when Herdr is available, with graceful unknown otherwise. Dry-run is entirely offline, projects occurrences only, and writes/sends nothing.
- README and docs/testing explain opt-in, source-file snapshotting, clock/sleep limits, grace on catch-up and queued delivery, cutoff, ambiguity of external execution, migration start/no overlap, single-watcher operation, and exact inspection/install/reload steps. Owner fleet configuration and production acceptance are performed by the requesting coordinator, not the designer's smoke test. Updating code requires a watcher restart; configuration reload alone cannot load new Python modules. Do not assert that unreleased local code is installed.

### 30.6 Required evidence

Write failing integration/recovery tests first and show the red result. Exercise the public CLI/parser and real scheduler-to-mailbox-to-watcher delivery seam, not only private calculations: valid/invalid config, offline dry-run zero writes and zero Herdr calls, daily next occurrences/cutoff, DST gap/fold and fallback parity, late start/sleep/forward/backward clock, one catch-up within grace, skip beyond grace, one pending message, prepared/file/log crash points, corruption and collisions, lock concurrency, reload/remove/disable/target edits, expiry while blocked/busy/typed input, recovery after unconfirmed delivery, immutable body/no unrelated-mail loss, sender/debt/audit metadata and no-timer regressions. All fake-Herdr call logs continue through tests/support.py safety checks.

Run both required Python suites and compileall; distinguish actual interpreter runs from grammar/fallback simulation. Review the final diff against this section and the public-content boundary. Designer live-smokes synthetic prompts only on a cheap Claude seat in a scratch fleet, including queue/hold, watcher restart/catch-up/deduplication, expiry and dry-run, and records results/limits below before committing. Never execute the requesting production snapshot or delivery job, change its fleet file, or restart its coordinator for acceptance.

### 30.7 Verification and limits

- **Red evidence.** Four public integration cases failed against the previous implementation before the feature was built: offline timer inspection was an unknown command, an undeclared target was not rejected, `watch --once` generated no scheduled request, and enqueue failure left no durable preparation to recover. The final timer module has 54 test methods. Additional independent synthetic reproductions caught timezone conversion/cache errors, journal-watermark replay, reversed audit chronology and inspection recovery; those findings were corrected rather than waived.
- **Recovery and privacy corrections.** Recovery validates a consistent journal and never overwrites a decided occurrence. Header-only ownership checks include the exact message id; no queued body or body digest is used for scheduling decisions, and journal fields remain metadata only. Recovered sends retain original enqueue time and logical order, including equal-second cases, so they neither lose the timer's owner debt nor settle a newer request to the owner. Decision-log recovery preserves its original timestamp, and inspection keeps newer skipped/stopped/error decisions visible. Removing the final timer and timer-bearing log-mode edits reconcile pending mail; a pristine history-free fleet keeps its former path.
- **Live setup.** The designer used an isolated temporary fleet and a Claude Code 2.1.310 seat with the [`haiku` alias](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/model-config#model-aliases), displayed as Haiku 5.5, under Herdr 0.9.5 and Python 3.9.6. The seat had no MCP servers and only the question tool enabled. The owner manually approved its initial directory-trust prompt. All prompt bodies were synthetic; no production task, job file, coordinator or fleet configuration was exercised.
- **Offline and catch-up checks.** A dry-run projected three fires with an unavailable Herdr binary and no changes to scratch filenames, file contents or modification times. A foreground checkout watcher then enqueued and delivered one eligible catch-up request from `owner`; the model's standalone acknowledgement was observed. After stopping and restarting that watcher, the same occurrence still had one send and one confirmed delivery, and timer inspection reported delivered progress.
- **Busy and blocked checks.** A synthetic busy stimulus initially exposed a test-observer timing error: the immediate state read preceded the working transition, so no timer pass was run. The corrected probe waited for an observed working state. It then verified one queued message and zero delivery while working, followed by one delivery of that original message at the next idle. A real question dialog separately held a due timer as blocked; after a short cutoff, the queued request expired once and stopped was logged once. Repeated passes neither delivered nor recreated it. No dialog answer or agent key was sent, and no unconfirmed message was resubmitted.
- **Cleanup and coverage limits.** The scratch watcher processes stopped and released their locks. The deliberately blocked test workspace, verified to contain only the created test seat, was closed without answering its question; evidence files were retained outside the repository. Live checks covered the log watcher and one-pass paths above. Corruption, concurrent crashes, replay, timezone transitions/fallback, other inbox delivery paths and configuration-reload combinations were tested synthetically, not by disturbing production or manufacturing live prompt failures. No actual Python 3.8 interpreter was available; native-backend execution and Python 3.8 grammar checks are separate evidence.
- **Publication boundary.** The 98-file candidate passes the known private-identifier/path and credential-pattern audit with zero findings. The local development `.herdr/` remains ignored and outside the public tree. The first three AGENTS.md rules are unchanged. No global plugin registration, installed watcher, owner fleet file, production credential setup, remote branch or release tag was changed by these acceptance checks. The candidate version is 0.15.0; installation and production acceptance remain separate actions.
- **Automated and independent checks.** The builder's final `/usr/bin/python3 -m unittest discover -s tests` run passed 947 tests on Python 3.9.6, and `/opt/homebrew/bin/python3.12 -m unittest discover -s tests` passed the same 947 on Python 3.12.13, with no failures, errors or skips. Coverage increased from 893 to 947 cases. `/usr/bin/python3 -m compileall -q herdr_fleet bin/herdr-fleet` passed. Independent review returned MERGE with no actionable source findings; its checks included recovery and debt-order reproductions, eight calendar parity cases, 17-schedule native-cache reuse/pruning, Python 3.8 grammar for 79 files, 20 shell blocks under bash and zsh, 13 TOML blocks, eight JSON blocks, 14 relative paths, diff checking and the public-content scan. The reviewer did not repeat the full suites, compilation or live tests; those are the builder/designer evidence above.

## 31. Cross-fleet mailbox ownership and observable invocation/runtime identity (0.16.0)

Field feedback identified four related support problems: a cross-fleet reply can be held outside the recipient's active inbox wait; queue output does not name the holding fleet; an owner fallback hides missing caller identity; and an installed update does not identify the code of an existing watcher. The owner approved fixing these issues and publishing/rolling out the verified release. This section supersedes section 20.8's source-owned queue and contact exclusion for newly routed cross-fleet sends. Existing messages are never automatically migrated or replayed.

### 31.1 One authoritative mailbox for a new cross-fleet send

- Keep selected-fleet local seats and `owner` local. For a resolved external contact or other live agent, discover its existing fleet from its authoritative agent record's cwd then foreground_cwd, using the current real-path discovery rules. Route automatically only when there is a unique named live agent and a valid existing destination fleet that declares that exact agent as a seat. Canonical destination name is that live Herdr name; a local contact alias is only a requested-name/display alias, never an invented destination seat. Do not infer an agent or fleet from a forwarded body, a matching cwd alone, focused pane, transcript, or prompt text. A declared local seat is not rerouted even if it temporarily works in another repository.
- The destination fleet owns the sole pending `inbox/<canonical-name>/new/` file, its mailbox lock, TTL and delivery. New automatic routes write no source `new/` file. Preserve sender, body, FYI/queue/urgent/TTL/reply metadata and one stable message id. Reserve/check ids against both involved inbox trees so neither an existing full id nor short id is overwritten or made ambiguous there. Honor a contact's resolved canonical agent identity even when its alias differs. A missing/ambiguous contact, unnamed agent, same-root target, invalid/missing destination fleet, or agent not declared there uses the existing source-owned behavior with a visible reason; it never creates a fleet file or manages the contact.
- Apply the ordinary send policy in the owning context: a plain idle/done send can submit immediately through the existing guarded delivery engine; `--queue` waits; FYIs retain deliberate deferral; working/blocked/unknown/typing targets remain queued; only existing explicit `--now` behavior may reach working input. `inbox --wait --as NAME` in the destination sees a queued FYI immediately without a native prompt. The destination watcher and pull share the destination mailbox lock, so one wins each delivery. Sender-side watcher cannot also deliver this message because it has no pending copy.
- Keep history and reply debt auditable on both sides using nonpending source history/receipt metadata and ordinary send/terminal log records. Persist validated origin metadata sufficient for later destination delivery/expiry/read/hook/kickoff outcomes to update the source log, without copying bodies outside the two existing inbox trees. Both logs use the canonical recipient consistently; requested aliases may be separate metadata. A routed send not yet delivered creates no reply debt; one delivery creates the corresponding debt; an exact reply or acknowledgement settles it in the ordinary way. Source receipts must not make a history copy count as pending/delivered before actual delivery. Failure to mirror a receipt is reported and never re-prompts. Validate destination/origin directories and metadata before any cross-fleet write; do not turn an arbitrary header path into directory creation outside an existing fleet.
- Destination queue creation is the authoritative success boundary. If it fails, report an error and do not fall back to a second source pending copy. If a later source receipt/log write fails, keep the original destination message, report its actual location, and do not create a replacement id. Preserve normal unconfirmed-prompt handling: never re-prompt automatically. Use a consistent lock order if any operation needs both roots; do not hold source and destination locks in opposite orders or invert timer-before-mailbox ordering.
- Compatibility: old pending queues remain owned by the fleet where their files already reside and follow their prior delivery/expiry semantics. Existing `cur/` history is not proof that a source pending copy was delivered; never import it back into a pending destination. No scan/replay of old cross-fleet bodies, automatic migration, production job or FYI wake-policy change. README explains the legacy queue distinction and how to inspect it without recommending blind redelivery.

### 31.2 Output names the sender, selected fleet and holding mailbox

- Human `send` output identifies the resolved sender, selected fleet and whether selection was explicit or discovered. Each result identifies the actual owning fleet/mailbox; when routed, show requested alias to canonical recipient and origin/destination. Preserve the unread-mail note before final per-target outcomes. Queued FYIs explicitly distinguish waiting for native delivery from availability to the recipient's pull inbox. Paths in normal operational output are appropriate; public examples use synthetic paths only. Dry-run shows the same proposed route/identity and writes no files, logs, locks or state.
- Human inbox output identifies the selected name/fleet, including empty results, so `owner` fallback cannot be mistaken for a coordinator inbox. Emit identity/fallback diagnostics on stderr for machine-readable JSON/count/hook modes; do not break JSON or count stdout. Hook errors and fallback remain silent as required by section 18.7. No new body interpretation or keys.
- Preserve legitimate owner use outside Herdr and from a verified shell pane. Within Herdr, a missing/unresolvable caller pane or failed identity lookup must not silently impersonate owner: fail closed with `--as NAME` guidance when caller identity cannot be established. Explicit `--as owner` remains available. Do not select the owner-focused pane or guess identity from cwd/session files. Distinguish an actual shell pane with no agent from an agent lookup error. Use only installed Herdr read APIs. A named caller resolves normally, and an unnamed agent still needs the existing unique contact match or explicit `--as`.

### 31.3 Fleet-scoped reply-ID errors

`--reply-to` stays scoped to the selected fleet's message files; do not perform a global or body-based search. On a missing id, retain exit 2 and zero new queue/log/prompt writes, name the selected fleet/mailbox namespace and explain that a forwarded request may belong elsewhere. Suggest `--fleet PATH` for its originating fleet, or a plain send when the message was only forwarded; state that a plain send does not retain exact reply linkage. Cover full and short IDs and preserve ambiguity errors. Shared `inbox.resolve` callers must retain useful operation-specific behavior.

### 31.4 Watcher launch identity and update drift

- Capture watcher launch package version and a best-effort full checkout commit at watcher acquisition. Read Git with `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`; never fetch, change history, or spawn Git every board refresh. Preserve PID/start/fleet and add optional launch identity to the existing lock. Release still verifies the owning PID. Old lock files are accepted with explicit unknown identity; do not infer their build from the current CLI.
- `status` human and JSON show the invoking CLI version/commit and the recorded watcher version/commit together, plus `current`, `stale` or `unknown` comparison. A differing known version/commit is stale; missing evidence is unknown, not current. Do not invent a commit when Git is unavailable; packaged installs still show their version. Cache immutable identity appropriately within a command/process. Keep fields additive for JSON compatibility.
- Stale output gives an explicit watcher-only reload instruction. It does not auto-restart, focus, close a pane, run a job or change configuration. README distinguishes installed CLI, watcher launch build and fleet-file reload. Version bump both declarations to 0.16.0; no release tag is required.

### 31.5 Required evidence and release workflow

Builder implements this numbered section in the shared working tree, with synthetic fixtures and public docs/help updates. Write regression cases that fail against the prior code for destination-owned contact and dynamic-agent sends, active destination wait, alias canonicalization, explicit-FYI no native wake, default idle delivery, working/blocked/unknown/typing holds, TTL/receipt/debt settlement, destination/source write failures, collision handling, two readers/watchers, legacy pending queues, and dry-run zero writes. Cover sender shell versus identity failure, explicit override, clean JSON/count/hook streams, actionable unknown-id errors and old/new watcher locks with current/stale/unknown build identities. Every fake Herdr scenario continues through tests/support.py safety invariants. Keep Python 3.8+ and dependency-free behavior; neither keys nor agent management gets a new path.

Run focused tests then full suites under the available Python below 3.11 and at/above 3.11, and compileall. Independent reviewer checks the final diff against this section and the whole public-content boundary. Designer live-tests synthetic cross-fleet queue/pull and native delivery on a cheap Claude seat in a disposable fleet, including an alias, a held delivery, an explicit sender, reply-ID guidance and watcher launch identity. Record exact results and limitations in an amendment here before commit. No production job is used as a test. After review and smoke checks, commit/push under the owner's current explicit authorization, install the exact published commit, and refresh the selected fleet watcher strips with guarded identity checks and `up LEAD --no-kickoff` then `tile`. Preserve coding-agent panes/sessions and fleet-file bytes; changing owner fleet files is unnecessary. Record deployment verification privately rather than putting internal fleet identities in public documents.

### 31.6 Launcher provenance: never silently run a development fallback

A late field report identified a related runtime-identity defect, verified against the generated launcher: `herdr plugin list --json` errors are suppressed and the development fallback is executed. Thus an installed-copy launcher can run a half-edited checkout merely because registry access was denied. This belongs to this release's runtime-provenance fixes; the owner has authorized fixing and publishing the issues.

- Normal launcher calls must execute the enabled `yarrasys.fleet` registration selected by exact plugin id. Distinguish successful registry lookup with no enabled installation from lookup failure, malformed output, missing executable, or invalid registered path. A lookup/selection failure prints a useful error/provenance diagnostic on stderr and exits nonzero. Never silently execute the checkout captured when `install-cli` was run. Preserve the actual registry command's failure rather than losing it through a shell pipeline; honor the configured Herdr binary consistently.
- `HERDR_FLEET_BIN` and the existing explicit `install-cli --dev` remain deliberate development opt-ins. Do not add a new automatic fallback or guess a managed installation. Normal successful stdout remains the invoked command's stdout, including valid JSON/count output. A diagnostic must not pollute those streams.
- Hooks retain their silent-failure contract: an unavailable/invalid registry, missing plugin/executable, or installed version without hook support yields exit 0 and empty stdout/stderr for `inbox --hook ...`. The fallback must never run there either. Do not alter unrelated command arguments or treat ordinary text containing `--hook` as a hook invocation.
- Update generated launcher text, help, README and setup instructions together. Keep atomic launcher replacement and refusal to overwrite unrelated files. Existing launchers require an explicit `install-cli` refresh after installing this release; include that in the rollout. Do not edit the live launcher during implementation.
- Add subprocess regressions using fake registry commands for permission/nonzero failure, malformed JSON, no enabled exact-id registration, missing executable, valid installed selection, explicit override/dev, clean JSON/count streams, hook silence and misleading similarly named plugins. Show that failures never execute a marker-writing development fallback. No real registry mutation or live jobs in tests. Designer verifies the generated launcher in a scratch directory, then refreshes the user's launcher during the authorized deployment.

### 31.7 Explicit source-file compatibility

An explicitly selected `--fleet PATH` remains a valid source configuration when it lives outside the conventional `.herdr/fleet.toml` location. Automatic cross-fleet ownership requires a conventional existing source root as well as the validated destination, so later paired receipts can be authenticated. If that source root cannot be validated, preserve the existing source-owned send/queue behavior and show the source eligibility reason; do not reject the otherwise valid selected file merely because automatic routing is unavailable. Do not create an external pending copy or a second authoritative queue. Add a regression using a valid custom source filename with no conventional source fleet file, covering successful local queueing and zero destination writes, alongside the normal routed case. This clarifies the compatibility boundary of 31.1 before publication.

### 31.8 Verification and limits

- **Automated evidence.** The final candidate passed 1,006 tests on Python 3.9.6 in 41.663 s and 1,006 on Python 3.12.13 in 37.161 s, with no failures, errors or skips; compilation exited 0. Coverage increased from 947 to 1,006 tests, including 59 focused section-31 methods. The initial six routing/identity regressions failed before implementation. Launcher selection/failure cases, equals-form hooks, malformed non-UTF-8 output, owner-local exact replies and custom source-file compatibility also have recorded red evidence before their fixes. Other new regressions were exercised green, without claiming that each was independently proven red. All safety call-log checks remained active. Python 3.8 grammar checks passed for 81 Python files and the generated bootstrap; no actual Python 3.8 interpreter was available.
- **Independent review and public boundary.** Independent review returned MERGE with no remaining actionable findings after checking the final compatibility delta. An inventory-specific count in this design was generalized before publication. The first three AGENTS rules and section 7 invariant text are unchanged. The 100-file current candidate has zero known private-content/credential-pattern findings; this is not a historical Git-content audit. Documentation shell/TOML/JSON syntax and relative links passed their recorded checks. Reviewer's independent pure/in-memory identity, receipt/debt and mocked launcher checks passed; it did not rerun the full suites, compilation or live acceptance.
- **Live setup and mailbox behavior.** The designer used two disposable scratch fleet configurations and a Claude Code 2.1.310 seat displaying Haiku 5.5 under Herdr 0.9.5. The previously owner-trusted scratch directory was reused; MCP servers were disabled and only the question tool was available. All requests were synthetic. An old-release FYI established the source-owned baseline. The candidate routed contact-alias and direct-name FYIs to one canonical destination pending file, made them available to the recipient's active inbox wait without waking the native model, and propagated one pull receipt to each log. The old source pending file remained byte-identical. Dry-run left both scratch trees unchanged; missing reply-id handling left queues/logs unchanged, and caller/count streams and unknown-caller failure matched the contract.
- **Native delivery and held input.** One plain alias request produced one native send/delivery and a standalone synthetic model acknowledgement. The first observer captured too short a visible tail in a tall pane and timed out; an idle recent-history read recovered the completed acknowledgement without resubmission. The exact acknowledgement command settled the corresponding debt in both logs. A second synthetic turn opened a real question dialog. A routed request remained in the destination queue across ordinary watcher passes while blocked, with no delivery, native key or dialog answer. The created workspace was closed only after stopping its manual watcher; no production job or agent was used as a test.
- **Launch identity and launcher selection.** A released scratch watcher with an old lock correctly appeared as unknown, without inferring its build from the invoking CLI. After stopping that foreground watcher, the candidate watcher recorded its launch package/version and checkout HEAD, and status reported a current comparison. The pre-commit checkout identity is not a digest of uncommitted bytes. A launcher generated only in a scratch directory selected the registered released installation rather than the development candidate. Fake denied/malformed registries failed visibly for ordinary commands, stayed silent for both hook spellings, and did not classify ordinary send text as a hook. The explicit development override still selected the candidate. The code registration was unchanged by these checks.
- **Scope and remaining limits.** Both manual watchers stopped and released their locks; only the created scratch workspace was closed, without answering its dialog. Concurrency, partial-write recovery, redirected metadata, stale-build comparisons, provider-independent state/presence holds and other delivery paths are automated evidence rather than additional production experiments. If a source receipt cannot be written, the single destination message is retained and the missing source mirror is reported; the plugin does not invent a replacement or relax paired-receipt validation. Missing upstream caller context cannot be repaired by guessing an author from cwd. Updating the installed registration, refreshing the real launcher and restarting selected watcher strips are separate deployment actions under the owner's authorization; their concrete inventory and verification stay private.

## 32. Switching a seat's agent: lost starts, named dialogs, self-handover (0.17.0)

Field evidence from 31 October 2026, the owner's request to fix it, and two coordinators' reports. A `swap` that moved one seat from Codex to Claude took about forty minutes, and the same day another fleet's coordinator swap needed a hand repair. In both cases the quit worked and the start did not: herdr's CLI waited out its default 30 s readiness timeout, answered `timeout: timed out waiting for agent startup`, and the plugin reported the seat `failed`, leaving a bare shell with no retry, no kickoff and no notification. One coordinator found the cause on screen. Codex leaves the terminal's mouse-tracking modes (1000/1002/1003/1006) switched on when it quits, so any mouse movement over the pane is typed into the shell as SGR reports. herdr's start command then lands behind them and runs as `10Mclaude`, which fails with command not found. Starting again by hand worked within a second. Earlier the same day, a quit of a `done` Codex seat was refused with `a dialog or a menu is on screen in PANE`, without saying what the screen showed or telling anyone. And because a seat may not restart itself, both teams had to borrow another seat, or write a controller script, to swap their orchestrator. This section answers all three, and the smaller requests that came with the reports (32.3, 32.4, 32.6, 32.7).

### 32.1 A start that times out is classified, retried once if it never ran, and announced

`start_in_pane` passes `--timeout START_READY_MS` (15000) to `herdr agent start`; the plugin polls for slower starts itself (step 2), so a lost command costs 15 s instead of 30. When herdr answers `timeout`, the plugin finds out what happened, reading only:

1. `agent get PANE` answers: the agent got past startup late. `idle`, `done` or `working` is a start; `blocked` or `unknown` goes to the read-only poll of 16.12 until `START_SLOW_S` (45 s) after the first start, then is handled as blocked at startup.
2. `agent get` fails, but `pane process-info` shows a foreground process of the seat's kind (`find_agent_process`): the agent is still coming up. Poll `agent get PANE` every 250 ms until `START_SLOW_S` after the first start, then as in 1. Not detected by then: `failed - its process runs in PANE but herdr has not seen it ready after 45 s; look at the pane`, and its kickoff is queued as in 32.6, since such an agent usually comes up later.
3. `agent get` fails and the pane is at a shell prompt (section 6) with no process of the kind: the command never ran, or ran garbled and failed. The plugin issues the same `agent start` once more, after a fresh process-info read that still shows the bare shell. The report says `started - in PANE (second attempt; the first start left a bare shell)`. If the second attempt also times out with a bare shell, the seat fails with `the start command did not run in PANE twice, or the agent exited at once; a quit agent may have left mouse tracking on there (README: troubleshooting); run herdr-fleet up NAME`. "Did not run" is an inference: an agent that runs and exits within the timeout (bad arguments, an auth error, a refused trust prompt) also leaves a bare shell and is started once more. A third start never happens automatically.
4. Anything else in the foreground: `failed` with its names, as today, and no retry.

A retry is only ever made in a pane that the section 6 check reports at a shell prompt, with no agent in herdr and no process of the kind, so invariant 5 holds and nothing is typed into a pane that hosts an agent. Garbled input in a bare shell is the shell's own business: the plugin does not read, clear or reset it.

Every seat that `up`, `restart` or `swap` leaves `failed` gets a herdr notification like a blocked start: title `Fleet: NAME did not start`, the detail as the body, sound `request`. A failed start after a quit ends its report line with `the pane is at a shell; run herdr-fleet up NAME`.

**What the plugin cannot do.** Switching the mouse modes off means writing to the terminal from inside the pane (`printf '\e[?1000l…'` run in the shell) or typing into it, and the plugin types into no pane but through `agent prompt` and the narrow key paths of 23.7 and 23.11. The README's troubleshooting section therefore gives the owner two remedies: the shell's own reset in a `precmd` hook (zsh) or `PROMPT_COMMAND` (bash), which switches mouse reporting off whenever a prompt is drawn, and, for a pane already affected, running that `printf` in it by hand. The proper fix is in herdr: reset the pane's input modes when its agent leaves, or before `agent start` types. The report to herdr upstream is the owner's to file.

### 32.2 A held quit names the dialog and tells the owner

When the guard (21.2) holds a quit because no prompt line is on screen, or a highlighted numbered choice stands where the line would be, `quit_agent` reads the guard again every 500 ms for up to `QUIT_SETTLE_S` (5 s), read-only, since a seat that has just turned `done` may still be repainting. If the line comes back empty the quit goes ahead. If not, the plugin reads `agent read NAME --lines 16` once and names the dialog by its first line, as 23.7 does for a quit that times out: `refused - a dialog or a menu is on screen in w1:p2 ("Approaching rate limits"); answer or close it there, then run the command again`. With no numbered choice on screen the line keeps today's words. The same refusal notifies the owner (`Fleet: NAME needs you`, sound `request`): only a person can answer it. Text in the input (`owner typing in`, `text in its input`) is refused at once as today. Delivery, the watcher and the board are unchanged.

### 32.3 `peek` on a working seat

herdr refuses `agent read --lines N` (default source `recent`) for a working seat whose history lives in the alternate screen: `agent_not_idle: cannot read 14 lines while … is working`. Both reports name this, and a working seat is the one an orchestrator most wants to look at. `Herdr.agent_read` gains `source` (`None`, `visible` or `recent`), and `peek NAME` gains `--source visible|recent`. Without `--source`, a `peek` refused with `agent_not_idle` reads `--source visible` once instead and prints `(working: the visible screen only)` to stderr before the text. The board's `p` key follows `peek`. `quit_agent`, `interrupt` and the board's blocked-dialog read keep their reads as they are: what they decide from is the current screen, and they never fall back silently (amended by 36: on a pane too short for their read, they read the viewport once).

### 32.4 A seat waiting in `inbox --wait` is shown as waiting

herdr reports a seat inside `inbox --wait` as `working`, and so did `status`, so a coordinator verifying a team could not tell a busy reviewer from one waiting for mail. While it waits, `inbox --wait` writes `.herdr/inbox/<name>/waiting.json` (`pid`, `since`, `timeout_ms`), and removes it when the wait ends, whatever the outcome. A file whose pid is not alive is ignored. While a fresh file exists and herdr says `working`, `status` prints `working (inbox wait 3m)` in the STATE column, `status --json` adds `"inbox_wait": {"since": …}` to the seat, and the board shows `waiting for mail 3m` in place of the terminal title. Nothing else reads it: delivery and the watcher still go by herdr's state, and the file holds no message content.

### 32.5 `--handover`: a seat restarted or swapped when its own turn ends

A seat may not restart, swap or quit itself, and that rule stays: the reports call it essential. But the seat that most often has to change agent is the orchestrator, and today it needs another seat or a hand-written controller to do it. `restart NAME --handover` and `swap NAME --handover` give it a visible helper instead.

- **Allowed only for the caller's own seat.** `--handover` naming any other seat, or run outside a seat's pane, is a usage error (`--handover is for your own seat; run restart NAME from another pane for any other`). Without `--handover`, the own-pane refusal gains `, or add --handover to be replaced when this turn ends`. A `swap NAME --handover` for a seat without drift prints `unchanged` and opens nothing. `--dry-run` prints the helper it would open and the operation it would run.
- **The helper.** The command opens the plugin pane entrypoint `handover` with `plugin pane open --placement tab --workspace <seat's workspace> --no-focus --cwd <repo>`. It passes `HERDR_FLEET_FILE` and `HERDR_FLEET_HANDOVER` (the operation, the seat, `wait_ms` and, if a note was given, the path of a copy under `.herdr/handover/<seat>-note.md`). It records the pane in the state file as `"handover": {"pane_id", "created_pane": true, "seat", "op", "started"}`, then returns at once: `sample-orchestrator: handover - waiting in w1:p9 (new tab); you are swapped when this turn ends, so end your turn now and do not start inbox --wait`. Only one handover per fleet at a time: a second one while a recorded handover pane is open is refused with its pane.
- **What the helper runs.** `herdr-fleet handover`, which only the pane runs. It prints what it waits for, runs `agent wait NAME --until idle --until done --timeout <remaining of wait_ms, default 3600000>`, then runs the recorded `op_restart` or `op_swap` for that one seat with its own invocation, so its pane is not the seat's and the own-pane rule holds. Every guard of 4.3, 6, 21.2, 32.1 and 32.2 applies unchanged. A seat refused because it turned `working` again, for example after a watcher delivery, is waited for again until the deadline. A blocked seat, a held quit or a failed start ends the helper.
- **How it ends.** On success the helper notifies `Fleet: NAME swapped` (or `restarted`) with the report as the body, drops its state record and closes its own pane with `pane close` (recorded `created_pane`, invariant 4). Otherwise it notifies `Fleet: handover of NAME stopped`, prints the report, and keeps its pane open until the owner presses Enter or closes the pane; the record is dropped then. `status` lists a recorded handover pane (`handover: swap sample-orchestrator waiting in w1:p9 since 11:40`) and drops a record whose pane is gone.
- **Bounds.** The helper is a foreground process in a visible pane that the owner can see and close, started only by the seat's explicit command (invariant 11). The watcher never starts one and never acts for one (invariant 12). The helper sends nothing to any agent except through the restart and swap paths, and never from a popup; those paths also name every live seat's pane after its seat (22.1), as they do from any pane. `tile` and the layout never see it, because it sits in its own tab.

### 32.6 A kickoff that cannot be sent waits in the seat's mailbox

A swapped coordinator that stopped at Claude Code's folder-trust prompt was answered by the owner within three minutes, but its kickoff never followed: `status` showed `(kickoff pending)` for two days, and the seat ran without its roster, inbox rules or state. A kickoff refused just after a start (the guard reading a prompt line that is not drawn yet, or a seat not yet idle) is lost the same way; the controller of this section's swap had to retry it by hand.

- When `up`, `restart` or `swap` start a seat and its kickoff cannot be sent (blocked at startup, or `safe_prompt` refused it: not idle or done, or held by the guard), the rendered kickoff, without its `{inbox}` block, is written to the seat's mailbox as a message from `herdr-fleet` with `queued_by: kickoff`, ahead of everything already queued. The report line says `kickoff queued #id: delivered when NAME is next idle or done`, and `status` shows `(kickoff queued #id)` in place of `(kickoff pending)`. An unconfirmed kickoff (`agent_prompt_stalled`, `timeout`) is not queued: it may have arrived (invariant 3).
- It is delivered by the existing engine and nothing else: the watcher, `deliver`, the flush before a `send`, a hook or the next kickoff. It goes only to an idle or done seat, through `safe_prompt` and the guard, like a `queue` message; it wakes the watcher's waiter, since it is not an FYI. Its text goes first and verbatim, the rest of the batch after it as usual. On delivery the plugin does what a sent kickoff does: `last_kickoff_at`, `blocked_at_startup = false`, a `kickoff` log entry with the original reason, and the settling of debts of 20.8.
- One queued kickoff per seat: a newer start or kickoff replaces it, and `kickoff NAME` drops it before sending its own. `--drop-inbox` drops it with the rest. It has no ttl.
- Invariants 11, 12 and 18 hold: the delivery mode comes from `queued_by`, set by how the message was written, and the watcher still only delivers queued messages to idle or done seats.

### 32.7 Help that names flags as flags

`inbox`'s help line reads `your own queued messages: --read, --wait, --count, --history` (it said `list, read, wait, count, history`, and `inbox count` is a usage error). `peek`'s help names `--source`.

### 32.8 Claude Code's grey suggestion is not typed text

Reported live by a coordinator on 31 October 2026 (0.16.0) and confirmed read-only on a seat of that fleet: after a turn, Claude Code shows a suggested next prompt in its empty input line, in faint text (`❯\xa0\e[2mcheck whether …\e[0m`). `pane read` prints it as plain text, so the guard reads the line as typed, and `send`, `deliver`, `kickoff` and `restart` to an idle Claude seat are held with `text in its input (PANE); press Ctrl-U there to clear it`, which is wrong advice: Ctrl-U, Esc and typing do not dismiss it. The owner's own typing is drawn at normal intensity.

- The guard reads `pane read PANE --lines 12 --format ansi`. It finds the prompt line as before, on the text with every escape sequence removed. For a kind listed in `seats.PROMPT_FAINT_PLACEHOLDER` (`claude`), it then drops every character drawn faint (from SGR 2 up to SGR 22 or SGR 0; colour parameters do not end it) before deciding. A line whose remaining text is empty, or is the kind's placeholder, is empty. Typed text followed by a faint completion still holds. Other kinds are unchanged, since a faint run there may be the owner's pasted text.
- The same reading applies wherever the prompt line decides something: `holds_text` for `interrupt`'s Ctrl-U cleanup of 23.11 (no Ctrl-U for a suggestion) and the quit window of 32.2. `prompt_line` stays pure and takes the ANSI text. Nothing of the line is kept, as before (invariant 19).
- `fake_herdr` serves `pane read --format ansi` from a scripted screen with escapes; without `--format ansi` it strips them as herdr does. Tests: a faint suggestion passes (send delivered, restart quits); typed text holds; typed text plus a faint tail holds; a faint `Try "…"` passes; a Codex line with a faint run is unchanged; `interrupt` presses no Ctrl-U for a suggestion.

### 32.9 Code and tests

`seats.start_in_pane` gains the timeout classification, the retry and the slow-start poll; `bringup.start_seat` the notification and the remedy; `seats.quit_agent` the guard window and the dialog line; `herdr.py` `agent_read(source=…)` and `--timeout` on `agent start`; `ops.op_peek` the visible fallback; `mailroom.op_inbox` the waiting file; `checks` (status) and `board` the waiting label; `bringup` and the delivery engine (`messages.prompt_mailbox`) the queued kickoff of 32.6; and a new `handover.py` holds both sides of 32.5, with the manifest's `handover` pane entry. `fake_herdr` gains `start_lost` (names whose first n starts answer `timeout` and leave the bare shell), `start_slow` (a process of the kind in the foreground for n `agent get` reads before the agent appears), `agent_not_idle` for `agent read` without `--source visible` on a working seat, `plugin pane open --placement tab`, and a screen that shows a menu for n guard reads. `support.py` checks that a second `agent start` to a pane follows a `process-info` read that showed a bare shell with no successful `agent get` in between and that no third follows, and that a handover helper runs restart or swap only for its recorded seat. Regression tests, each failing on 0.16.0: a lost first start retried once and briefed; a lost second start failed with the remedy, notified, and no third start; a slow start waited for and briefed; a slow start past the deadline; a timeout with a blocked agent; a quit held by a menu that clears within the window; a quit held by a menu that stays, with its first line in the report and a notification; text in the input still refused at once; `peek` of a working seat falling back to the visible screen, `--source` both ways; the waiting file written and removed on reply, timeout and interrupt, a dead pid ignored, and the label in status, JSON and board; `--handover` refused for another seat and outside a seat, the helper opened in a tab with its env, a second handover refused, the helper waiting for idle, swapping, briefing, notifying and closing its pane, waiting again after the seat turned working, and stopping on a blocked seat, leaving its pane open; a blocked start and a refused kickoff each queueing the kickoff first in the mailbox, the watcher delivering it once the seat is idle with the state and log updated and debts settled, a newer kickoff replacing it, `kickoff NAME` dropping it, and an unconfirmed kickoff not queued. Dry runs change nothing. Version 0.17.0 in both declarations; README (swap, troubleshooting) and AGENTS.md architecture updated together.

### 32.10 Amendments from the build

Built into the working tree on 31 October 2026 (version 0.17.0), with 32.8 added during the build. The designer accepted items 1 to 8 before the code was written.
